ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e2988	Mrs Bundesminister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e3003	You are quite right, ladies and gentlemen, the longer-term development of the Austrian university area is, of course, very central – not only because it is so defined in the government programme, but because it is, of course, a central point in science and higher education policy. That is why we have selected people who also have a European context, but of course also know the Austrian university landscape well. Of course, when we talk about the “Wissenrat” and this higher education plan, we also need questions such as: What's the matter with you? What's our goal? What about the university plan at all? – It is a question of simply expanding the historical territory more specifically, and this requires a coordinating higher education plan as a rolling overall planning of the tertiary sector. In particular, in addition to the elimination of double-tracks, the subject balance between universities clearly also serves the focus formation and profile sharpening, so that we can also become visible internationally with our strengths, with our excellence. The results are three plans for the future further development of the university area, which need to be continuously developed. On the one hand, this is the funding of the study ground. This means that a certain sum will be paid per course of study. This also means, of course, that this funding is used to determine how many places of study are financed by public funds and where resources flow. This clearly determines the current capacities and future construction. The other plans are the construction plan and the major infrastructure plan for research, which is to be able to use the large-scale research equipment together better.

This too is an important project of our cooperation, because it is a matter of preparing the students better for the decision: What should they do best to study? What are they best suited for? – Unfortunately, we have the situation that around 60 percent of the students go into only 10 percent of the subjects. We still need a lot of action to improve study advice. This too is an important concern to me, together with my colleague, Mr Schmied.

The political groups are free to divide this speaking time into one or two speakers. There is also agreement that actual corrections will only be called after the television broadcast. This forward strategy should solve what was even promised ten years ago, namely Europe, the European Economic Union in the sense of making it one of the economically strongest and most competitive regions in the world – that was the real promise. We stayed far behind, we didn't solve it. Indeed, the crisis within the euro area also has to do with the fact that individual countries, especially peripheral countries, have not been able to fully meet the requirement of competitiveness. Here, I think there are some very interesting ideas: Mario Monti, with his approach to improving the internal market, would be a concept of how one and a half percent of additional economic growth could be generated each year. The proposal of the Task Force Herman Van Rompuys would be the right answer – at least a big step towards it – how to manage a stronger economic coordination with better control of individual things – the statistical central offices, the improvement of control of hedge funds and rating agencies, the high frequency trading and all these things – while at the same time improving the competitiveness of more research, more innovation. I believe that we must not forget, however, that the growth and stability pact naturally requires that the budgets be balanced. This has not been the case in individual countries, and we also have to do our homework in this respect, but of course we are thanks to God in a much better situation. I believe, however, that we must be careful not to draw the wrong conclusions from it. At the moment, the ECB, the European Central Bank, is practically financing over € 130 billion of the liquidity of Irish banks. In my opinion, it was a big mistake that the Irish guaranteed everything in this famous September 2008: not only the deposits, but all the commitments the banks have ever entered into in Ireland. The result can be seen today. I therefore believe that Chancellor Merkel is right that private creditors must be involved in any way for the future; no way is going by that! As you do, you need to be quietly and properly negotiated, confidential, and not just all in the public in the form of a cabophony. And one thing will not go: that, for example, the British government, which, according to my information, is the largest creditor for Irish bonds and Irish banks, believes to get away from it. And – honestly – also € 7 billion, which are now thought to be, is a rather inadequate contribution, and I hear that this is also being called into question by Eurosceptics within the UK. So it can't happen that the biggest creditors ultimately debt themselves at the expense of the euro screen. In November of 2009, we took over the Presidency of the Security Council and tried to get something going that will leave a footprint in future during further negotiations, namely the protection of civilians in armed conflicts. Why is that so important to us? We see worldwide that human rights violations are repeated and crimes committed against civilians are also being committed where the UN is active as a leading organisation. That's why it's necessary. We have achieved our resolution that in the future there will be a mandate for every United Nations mission to protect civilians and that the UN and the Headquarters in New York should be reported so that the Security Council can respond. We have achieved this by means of resolution 1894, and for this the international community of states has also thanked us, last but not least, Foreign Minister Hillary Clinton, in whom I was a guest yesterday. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and representatives of the SPÖ.]] Secondly, I would like to mention that on a very sensitive issue, we have set another particular priority in the Security Council. We have achieved that we have appointed an ombudsman to organise a legal representation for persons on this list. We have now found recognition in Afghanistan, where this is a particular problem. All in all, the community of states has thanked us for this. [[Consistency with the FPÖ and the Abg. Linder.]] There we have to say: Dear friends, if you want Turkey's membership, we'll make it clear that it will fail at the Veto Österreichs! We don't want her! This is still a unanimous matter, if, then, the Constitution changes, sees if you get a majority, but Turkey's accession will not be there because Austria is against its veto! – This would stop the negotiation process so that fair, transparent, clear and dignified conditions would be created, so that neither Turkey nor the individual states, nor our citizens would go around the nose, and thus the way would be free for a reasonable partnership outside of membership. Chapter two, the EU per se. – Mr Schüssel said in his statement of entry that we must not go back, but we must continue along the path. I wonder, What is the path of success that we need to take? Centralism, bureaucracy, steering, transfers, subsidies have brought us into this problematic situation in which we are today. Look at it: Which states are causing us headaches? Which countries are in trouble? – These are exactly the subsidy and transfer states. These are Greece, Spain, Portugal and Ireland, and these are precisely those countries that have received the entire transfer payment since their entry into the EU. The sum of all the transfers we make as net payers has gone to these countries, whether from the title of the Cohesion Fund or the Regional Fund. There, of course, we have created economic inhomogenesities and bubbles. In Spain, for example, there has been a huge overinvestment in infrastructure through a completely crazy and unwarranted construction of roads and motorways. There was an enormously inflated construction industry subsidized by EU funds. This one has made the bubble of real estate, which has expanded the financial sector, but which we do not only see there. This has led countries such as Greece to neglect budgetary discipline. Aware of being a member of the euro zone, where nothing will happen, it was possible for Greece to be indebted to this extent. Only through the trust of the creditors that the Germans, Austrians and Dutch will finally pay, was it possible that the spread, i.e. the distance of interest payments between Greece and Germany, did not remain zero, but rather minimal. ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e10869	Mr President. Mr. Foreign Minister! Ambassador! The integration of the Balkan region into the European integration process has contributed significantly to political and democratic stability in south-eastern Europe and to the impressive economic recovery process of the region in recent years. Thus, the approach of the Western Balkans to the European Union and the international community has been the most important peace project in Europe since the end of the Franco-German inheritance and the overcoming of the East-West conflict. The EU and its Member States’ Stabilisation and Association Agreements to be consulted and to be concluded today, on the one hand and the Republic of Serbia on the other, should further accelerate and consolidate this positive development since the end of the Balkan wars in the 1990s. Since 1999, the stabilisation and association process with Bosnia-Herzegovina, Croatia, the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia, the former Yugoslav Republic of Macedonia and Albania, and from 2005 onwards with Serbia and Montenegro, has opened up new prospects for a wide-ranging partnership with the countries of south-eastern Europe. Like my previous speakers, I also do not want to mention that this process did not always go straight and without breaks. For example, negotiations with Serbia as legal successors of the State Federation of Serbia and Montenegro were suspended from 2006 to 2007 due to a lack of cooperation with the International Criminal Court for the former Yugoslavia – the so-called Hague Tribunal – and due to the Serbian attitude in the Kosovo question. With Serbia’s signalled willingness to cooperate on the dialogue between Belgrade and Priština, which led to the UN General Assembly’s Kosovo Resolution of September this year, with the 2007 Declaration of Neutrality, Security Policy Integration in the Partnership for Peace, with the exemplary minority council law of August 2009, on which 19 national minorities can choose self-government bodies for the fields of culture, education and the media, with the end of the visa requirement for Serbian citizens and the Serbian EU accession application of December 2009, a very dynamic and promising approximation of Serbia’s approach to the EU is outstanding for both sides. The SAA process, which is now being reintroduced, aims to strengthen democratic preparedness, to help improve the social and economic situation and to motivate Serbian politics and civil society in its reform efforts. These reform efforts will be measured by us, among other things, in the progress in the fight against corruption and organized crime and in the hopefully soon arrest and extradition of the fleeting war criminals Mladić and Hadžić. Mr Schüssel has already referred to it: There is, of course, also a real economic interest in Europe and, above all, Austria. In the period 2000 to 2008, the Austrian external trade volume has almost tripled with an export increase of more than 150 percent. After the dramatic import and export collapses in 2009, economic researchers expect that the volume of foreign trade will already reach the level of 2008 in the foreseeable future. However, the region is not only an important trading partner, but also an interesting investment location. Austria is one of the top international investors and is in Croatia, Bosnia and Herzegovina and Serbia undisputed, and I think we will continue to expand this economic situation – especially in the environmental and energy fields. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Social democracy agrees with the present Stabilisation and Association Agreement with the Republic of Serbia in the hope of a positive economic and social development and a sustainable peaceful and democratic development for the benefit of all citizens throughout Europe.

Let's take a look at what's already there: The Council of Europe is the central, already praised place. There is the European Court of Human Rights, which was founded under the European Charter of Human Rights and which monitors compliance with it. This is actually the central body for the monitoring of human rights. There, too, anyone who has exploited the national legal system can turn to it. Then, of course, there is the UN Human Rights Council with various subcommittees, such as the World Conference against Racism. In the EU itself, there is a Commissioner for Human Rights who preserves human rights. But also in the OSCE, the Organisation for Security and Cooperation in Europe, there is the ODIHR. This does not have to do with Odin or with Odal, as you may believe, but it is the abbreviation of the English term for organization of democratic institutions and human rights. This also makes it relatively clear what this organization is doing. And because all of this is not enough, there is now the European Fundamental Rights Agency. Everyone should take a picture of how important this is. The result is that, as we have already heard today, the European Parliament believes that there must be a 6 percent increase in the budget in the coming year, probably to create some agencies, the number is currently 34. We of the Freedom Party therefore believe – even if there is the organization, and if it is based in Vienna – that such a bureaucratic, unnecessary Hydra must be clearly said No. That is why we will under no circumstances agree with this agreement. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] I would personally say: If I could, I'd vote with no three times. In short, another chapter, which is not insignificant, to the agreement with the Republic of Albania on mutual assistance in the event of disasters and serious accidents. First of all, the word “on the other side” alone is a bit bizarre. Do we really expect to ask for aid from Albania in the event of accidents in Austria that we need Albanian aid in the event of accidents? - So I don't expect this to happen seriously. Here one could be honest and perhaps conclude an agreement on aid to the Republic of Albania in the event of accidents. Secondly, Albania is not exactly our neighboring state – on the road about 15 hours away. I think it is not necessarily our own task to provide assistance in the Republic of Albania. This should be regulated a little bit in the neighborhood area. For Albania, it would be useful if the Greeks, in my opinion, also the Serbs – despite the old friendship – or the Italian help that can come in two and a half hours, but not Austria. That is why we will not agree to the really pointless agreement either. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e12451	Mr President. Minister! High house! According to media reports, there are about 15 million illegal immigrants in Europe. According to Schlepper report of the Federal Ministry of the Interior, almost 50 percent of all illegal immigrants in Austria come to the country via Italy. Another problem is that the protection of the external borders of Schengen, which is the responsibility of the Member States concerned, is often insufficient. How else can it be explained that, for example, in Austria, almost 16 000 asylum applications were submitted in 2009, although our only Schengen external borders are located in Vienna-Swechat and at the other five airports? The situation will not be improved even in the course of the Schengen enlargement. On the contrary, it is to be feared that by opening the border, even more illegal immigrants are flocking to the country across the eastern borders of Austria. It is therefore only logical to consider a common restrictive immigration policy at European level. As Schengen borders are common external borders, border protection should also be operated jointly. The EU Frontex agency would be more than predestined for such use, but it would have to be equipped with extended powers and resources. Today it is – or should it be – that Frontex is a border harmonisation authority. There should be an exchange of information on the databases of the national authorities, it should be possible to synchronize them. All of this sometimes doesn't work. We have therefore tabled an application that has the intention that the European States should primarily join forces towards a more restrictive immigration policy in the EU. There is a supplementary request; we think it is too soft, we have rejected it and we will also reject it. I would like to say that you must not forget – this must be remembered again and again – what we are actually talking about here. Business with immigration, migration and so-called refugee care is, in many cases, a great business for the caregivers. It is therefore clear that the criticism of the usual practice or the questioning of the system and the consequences is not inhuman or hetceric, but is simply and simply business harmful. This is a hard distribution disc. sion: Who gets a thing why, and who pays when and where how much? – Ethnic, religious or cultural considerations do not play a role here in many cases. The ideas are based only on the interests. But, of course, you have to make a few things clear. For example, people are drawn, and they stay here when everything is legal. If this basic condition does not exist, if it has not been legalised, then it must also be acted on; in German this means that it is also postponed. If there are real disadvantages, they have to be eliminated; but if they are only suspected, then you have to make that clear. Once again, in conclusion: A coherent consensus across all states would be worthy of support that there should be a closed restrictive immigration policy at European level, whose expression would be a common authority, the Frontex, which would also be equipped with appropriate competences.

He knows what he's talking about, he's coming from the university himself, he was a minister of science – and he is therefore fully committed to regulating university access. Mr Rosenkranz pointed out that Austria was pumping a lot of money into education. Yes, that is true, Austria is pumping a lot of money into education, and the question is: Is the output right? And that's where we see a problem. We invest a lot in education, but we need a better output in education, and we already have to ask ourselves the question: Can I achieve a better output through more money alone? My answer is: No, we need better conditions. In addition to more money, we also need better framework conditions to ensure planning for universities. ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e16780	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! There are two ways to deal with the past: One kind of thing is as the Czech Republic does, that it says, past is past. A final line has to be drawn sometime. The past is a sum of errors and injustices, and if we try to roll around in them and balance them, then we will not get anywhere. We have to live today, not think of yesterday, and therefore those who have suffered injustice in the past are wrong. And end. – That's the one way. The other option is the one that we have chosen, Germany in particular, that it means that the past never leaves you out, you have to always put up with the past. The victims of the past are an eternal reminder, and we are also obliged to remember their – not eternally, but very long; at least as long as a victim lives – and, as far as possible, to compensate them, to comfort them and to do good what has been wrong. We from FPÖ have decided on this path. One of the principles of our legal system and our moral understanding is – at least I see it so – that we allow equal treatment to take place, that we treat all groups of victims, of unjust treatment, and that they are equally valuable to us. We are not legal successors and no intellectual heirs of National Socialism, and yet we feel responsible for what happened at the time, and still do so financially. We are perhaps even more spiritual descendants of the Slovenian, Croatian or Yugoslav partisans, because a part – at least in the House – repeatedly explains that he feels committed to the basic antifascist consensus. And this basic antifascist consensus also includes Tito-Partisans, Stalinists and so on. Nevertheless, although we may even have a spiritual succession, it is obviously very, very difficult that we remember the victims of this antifascism at our borders, within the borders and precisely beyond the borders. Mr Kurzmann has tabled a motion for a resolution which, I believe, should be clear and, in my opinion, in what we have in terms of values and values. He wrote: "The Federal Minister for European and International Affairs is invited to intervene on the Republic of Slovenia, to guarantee further historical correct processing of the partisans' crimes, to prosecute and punish the guilty, and in addition to creating a worthy memorial to the victims by creating, on the one hand, the corresponding historical consciousness, on the other hand, the building of memorials for the victims." So he did not even ask for reparation and compensation, but he only demanded an appropriate and relatively cost-effective commemoration. This, however, does not seem to be possible and does not represent a basic consensus in our Republic, since there has been a majority report in the Committee, which was adopted by a majority after this request was rejected, where nothing is in it. I am entitled to quote this majority report in its summary section, because it gives a good insight into the way in which this majority is working. I quote: “In this sense, the Committee welcomes the development in many countries, for example, on the occasion of the discovery of mass graves or the opening of archives, the increasing willingness to discuss public and scientific matters and the related critical media coverage. The focus should be on the dignity of the victims in order to give them names, faces and voices again in public.” [[Abg. May. Stefan: That's amazing!]] Mr President. I do not think I need to comment on why we can only do one thing in this case, namely reject this report. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e17610	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! First of all, I admit that the two laws that we will now decide were not easy birth. They were a serious birth, and that for the very simple reason – and we have the problem very often – that there is black sheep in one area, that there is just too criminal energy in an area, criminal tax evasion, professional tax fraud, but we have to pay very much attention to the fact that we do not meet with the lawn mower also those who are not. If we are honest, we must admit that the tax laws that we constantly adopt are so complex and so complicated that those concerned are only able to apply these laws by means of a constantly growing profession, namely tax advisors. Therefore, despite tax advisors, small and medium-sized enterprises may simply also experience errors that have nothing to do with tax fraud at all. Therefore, the art was where truly professional, criminal tax frauds are at work to use the full hardness of the law. But I also said: We need a anti-fraud law, but we don't need anti-subsidy law. That was my formulation. Therefore, the balance: On the one hand, all the hardness of the law for those who are professional with counterfeit documents, with fake documents, with fake transactions, with pre-tax fraud and so on, so really in criminal intent do not pay taxes, and another procedure for those tens of thousands of small and medium-sized enterprises that are simply over-exposed in the complex matter or ignore something at speed. I think that this balance has been relatively good with these two laws: On the one hand, we have introduced a new state of action in financial criminal law, namely tax fraud, with massive consequences, and on the other hand, we have tried to find a solution for small and medium-sized enterprises, which today really complain in part about how tax audits are taking place in practice, by means of the instrument of a reduction surcharge – 10 percent plus the tax debt and thus a penalty exemption. This applies within certain limits, i.e. if the amount per year does not exceed € 10 000 and in total does not exceed € 33 000. So we have tried to find the balance in it by massively dealing with the serious fraud, but on the other hand, we do not want to burden and insure those who in themselves really correctly calculate their taxes, but here and there a mistake happens. It is also very positive that we really have a massive package of measures against blackwork in the construction sector, with many comprehensive measures. According to me, 18 more speakers speak, so I will not go into all the details, that's what we've split up a little bit. I mean, this too is a very important approach. A third priority is that we should extend the KIAB, which is actually responsible for illegal foreign employment and is very successful there, to a kind of financial police force with very far-reaching powers. It is therefore an overall package that – once again – was not easy to bind. However, we are very optimistic that we will be really successful with this package. I'm just giving an example: We have taken very consciously and well into the budget path of the next four years additional revenue from this fight against fraud from €100 million in the first year up to €400 million in the fourth year. So we say quite consciously, self-confidently: We want to put in place these measures. In the sense of fiscal justice, we really have to intervene in a truly massive way where, today, really criminal energy is available in the tax area. You need to see that object-iv. On the other hand, once again: Liberation, debureaucratisation and impunity for small businesses, which are not tax frauds, which simply happen to errors. In this sense, I very much hope for broad approval.

It is very common to deal with organised crime, usually cross-border. We have now been forced to introduce a crime record. We welcome this extremely, because it is precisely these cases, previously only mentioned in § 38 with jurisdiction, where it is a matter of industrialism, where it is a matter of indifference, where it is a matter of using force. I think it is right to view this as a crime state and thus significantly increase the criminal threat, up to five years or four years plus 1.5 million euros in fine. New rules have also been introduced in response, I would say now, to § 108 of the Enforcement Act, namely a new 10 per cent reduction supplement, which is regulated in § 30a, when it comes to € 10 000 in one financial year or € 33 000 in total. This is, as it were, a free purchase of financial liability. My colleague stupidly said that it can also happen that one makes mistakes. This is clearly and clearly regulated, and it is not necessary to criminalise it. This is also true in § 146, in the simplified system of penalties. The penalty amount has been increased so that you can now re-enter from € 1 450 to € 2 000 possible fines. This relieves the apparatus, if I may call it that way, and it has now been re-created for this device. That's how the proverbs finally get a branch office. This means a much better organization. The opening decisions have now been re-regulated, as 50 percent of the appeals have generally been made in respect of an incorrect notice of initiation. We have also re-regulated the treatment of the illegal manufacture of tobacco in factories. There has so far been a gap in the legislation. We had this in my knowledge at least twice in Austria, once very close to the airport in Maria Ehrend, where you found yourself by chance. There was a penalty gap so far. This has now been added to § 43 with a criminal framework of up to € 100 000. That too is very welcome. There is, however, an area I do not find God. I have already presented this to the Secretary of State, including the Minister responsible, Vice-Cancellor Pröll. It is a new regulation in the field of piracy. That is where we are again in the area of organised crime. This is not about the individual traveler who buys something cheap somewhere and thus violates intellectual property. The product piracy law is to be applied when organized crime is at work, large quantities are introduced. According to the Financial Criminal Act, it is also a offence when it is referred to as such in other laws. This is the case in the Product Piracy Act, but without success. If you read the reports, the product piracy reports to Parliament, which exist every year, you will find: There is not yet a single case that would have been prosecuted under financial law. It is formulated in such a way that de facto does not occur in practice. I believe that the legislator should go on to represent this really as a fined offence, and at least make a new formulation in the Financial Penalty Act. I think it's unbearable. In particular, I am surprised that no official crime has been created in the context of product piracy. Unfortunately, we have not yet managed to break through with this approach. Mr State Secretary, you have until the next novel. I mean, it would be wise and reasonable if you also brought that into the Financial Penal Code. The other rules mean an intensification for those who save themselves unlawfully. This is also a protection for all those who in this Republic give good and honest tax returns and pay their taxes brutally and honestly. That is why it is very welcome.

ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e19837	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! Under item 2 of the agenda, we have held a very wide-ranging discussion on cooperation with and integration of Serbia into the EU structures and have also decided to do so together. Now, under item 13 of the agenda, we are dealing with an agreement to avoid double taxation. Some of my previous speakers have already mentioned the essential points in this agreement. However, the term ‘double taxation’ is somewhat misleading because it is actually about avoiding double taxation. The double taxation agreement essentially follows the rules of the OECD pattern agreements and includes points of transparency and commitment. Ladies and gentlemen, mobility and the use of workers has increased considerably in recent years. Workers are increasingly relocated to other countries or are employed in other countries for longer or shorter periods of time. The provisions of the Double Tax Agreement, which also regulate the income of self-employed workers, are therefore becoming increasingly important for these groups of persons. The aim of the agreement is also to protect international economic activity and cross-border investment from double taxation. In the external relations of our country, the Republic of Serbia is gaining more and more Meaning. Austria is the largest foreign investor in Serbia. The volume of trade between Serbia and Austria was already approaching the €900 million mark in 2008 and is again heading towards growth this year. Austria exported €95 million in the first quarter of 2010, the import volume is €55 million. The exchange of information between the authorities provided for in § 27 of the Agreement is in line with the latest OECD position. Ladies and gentlemen, this double taxation agreement is to be welcomed, it leads to more tax justice and greater transparency. I'll ask you for your consent. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2010-11-18-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00085_d8e20899	I will resume the interrupted session and announce the results of the election: Members cast: 161, of which: 157. Hievon was in favour of university professor Dr. Michael Holoubek 109 votes, Dr. Johannes Hock 37 votes, Dr. Lilian Hofmeister 9 votes and Dr. Johann Ferk 2 votes. The absolute majority of valid votes are 79. Thus, the proposal of the National Council for the appointment of a member of the Constitutional Court to Dr. Michael Holoubek. [[General condolences.]] I should like to congratulate Mr. University Professor Dr. Michael Holoubek on his election and wish him both a good hand and the necessary mind for the performance of his office in the next few years and decades.
